when I'm watching a video for my business assignments, I can start out listening, but after a few minutes my mind is on charm designs or order ideas and I realize I missed half of what was said.\n- situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: During longer classes or vendor meetings, I keep finding reasons to get up, like checking my inventory box, refilling water, or walking Like in class, or even when I’m watching a video for my business assignments, I’ll start out paying attention, but after a few minutes my mind drifts to charm designs or order ideas. Then I realize I missed a big part of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not actually doing anything, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing. If I’m supposed to sit still and listen, I really notice it—I feel like I need to move or shift around.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes. In class discussions or group critiques, I’ll jump in with an answer before the other person is even finished talking. Then I realize I kind of guessed what they were going to say, and I feel embarrassed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or irritable?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big ongoing thing. Anxiety, maybe here and there, but not something I’ve paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really pay attention to whether I talk more than other people. I might sometimes, but it’s not something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Waiting my turn to talk is hard, especially in critiques or if I’m trying to ask a question after class. I get really impatient and kind of shift around, and sometimes I cut in before I even mean to. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big ongoing thing. Anxiety, maybe here and there, but not something I’ve paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really pay attention to whether I talk more than other people. I might sometimes, but it’s not something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in line or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Waiting my turn to talk is hard, especially in critiques or if I’m trying to ask a question after class. I get really impatient and kind of shift around, and sometimes I cut in before I even mean to. Lines are hard too, like financial aid lines—I just feel restless waiting.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to engage in quiet leisure activities, like reading or drawing, without feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Not really. Even if I’m just sitting there, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing, like I need to move. It’s worse when I’m supposed to be sitting still and listening, but even with quiet stuff, I start feeling like I need to get up or shift around.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as being overly active or having a lot of excess energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, apparently I was. My mom still has notes from elementary school where teachers said I was always getting out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and kind of rushing through group activities even when I knew what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Were you often getting into physical fights or having physical altercations as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being in fights like that, and I don’t think that was a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused or following instructions not just in school, but also when managing your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part fine, but then later I’ll realize I missed something important. Like I forgot to attach a form, or I didn’t finish the last part of an order note. It happens enough that it causes problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even when I’m not actually doing anything, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing. It happens enough that it causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of disorganization, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, or have they varied in severity over the years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been there since I was little. Like I said, my mom still has those notes from elementary school where teachers wrote that I was always out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and rushing through group stuff even when I understood the work. It’s changed some over time, but it wasn’t something that just started recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks not just in school, but also when working on your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part, but then later I find out I skipped something important, like attaching a form or finishing the last part of an order note.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your accessories shop, similar to what you experience in school?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

I don’t remember being in fights like that, and I don’t think that was a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused or following instructions not just in school, but also when managing your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part fine, but then later I’ll realize I missed something important. Like I forgot to attach a form, or I didn’t finish the last part of an order note. It happens enough that it causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of disorganization, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, or have they varied in severity over the years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been there since I was little. Like I said, my mom still has those notes from elementary school where teachers wrote that I was always out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and rushing through group stuff even when I understood the work. It’s changed some over time, but it wasn’t something that just started recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks not just in school, but also when working on your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part, but then later I find out I skipped something important, like attaching a form or finishing the last part of an order note.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your accessories shop, similar to what you experience in school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I know I miss steps sometimes, but with tiny details or careless mistakes specifically, I’d have to think about it more. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with organization, restlessness, and impulsivity are causing problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. Even if I’m just sitting there, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing, like I need to move. It’s worse when I’m supposed to be sitting still and listening, but even with quiet stuff, I start feeling like I need to get up or shift around.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as being overly active or having a lot of excess energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, apparently I was. My mom still has notes from elementary school where teachers said I was always getting out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and kind of rushing through group activities even when I knew what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Were you often getting into physical fights or having physical altercations as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being in fights like that, and I don’t think that was a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused or following instructions not just in school, but also when managing your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part fine, but then later I’ll realize I missed something important. Like I forgot to attach a form, or I didn’t finish the last part of an order note. It happens enough that it causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of disorganization, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, or have they varied in severity over the years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been there since I was little. Like I said, my mom still has those notes from elementary school where teachers wrote that I was always out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and rushing through group stuff even when I understood the work. It’s changed some over time, but it wasn’t something that just started recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks not just in school, but also when working on your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part, but then later I find out I skipped something important, like attaching a form or finishing the last part of an order note.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your accessories shop, similar to what you experience in school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I know I miss steps sometimes, but with tiny details or careless mistakes specifically, I’d have to think about it more. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with organization, restlessness, and impulsivity are causing problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. Like, I missed some required steps for my program, and now my financial aid is kind of at risk because of it. And with my shop projects, people have started depending on me less because I don’t always follow through the way I’m supposed to. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in both your schoolwork and your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure if I’d say that happens often in both. I know I miss steps or forget parts of instructions sometimes, but with details or careless mistakes specifically, I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having a really hard time keeping things organized. I’m trying to manage my schoolwork and also a little accessories shop, and everything just ends up in piles—supplies, due dates, receipts, assignment notes. I look at it all and I don’t know what to do first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty often. Like in class, or even when I’m watching a video for my business assignments, I’ll start out paying attention, but after a few minutes my mind drifts to charm designs or order ideas. Then I realize I missed a big part of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not actually doing anything, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing. If I’m supposed to sit still and listen, I really notice it—I feel like I need to move or shift around.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes. In class discussions or group critiques, I’ll jump in with an answer before the other person is even finished talking. Then I realize I kind of guessed what they were going to say, and I feel embarrassed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or irritable?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get annoyed sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big ongoing thing. Anxiety, maybe here and there, but not something I’ve paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really pay attention to whether I talk more than other people. I might sometimes, but it’s not something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in line or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Waiting my turn to talk is hard, especially in critiques or if I’m trying to ask a question after class. I get really impatient and kind of shift around, and sometimes I cut in before I even mean to. Lines are hard too, like financial aid lines—I just feel restless waiting.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to engage in quiet leisure activities, like reading or drawing, without feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Not really. Even if I’m just sitting there, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing, like I need to move. It’s worse when I’m supposed to be sitting still and listening, but even with quiet stuff, I start feeling like I need to get up or shift around.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as being overly active or having a lot of excess energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, apparently I was. My mom still has notes from elementary school where teachers said I was always getting out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and kind of rushing through group activities even when I knew what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Were you often getting into physical fights or having physical altercations as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being in fights like that, and I don’t think that was a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused or following instructions not just in school, but also when managing your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part fine, but then later I’ll realize I missed something important. Like I forgot to attach a form, or I didn’t finish the last part of an order note. It happens enough that it causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of disorganization, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, or have they varied in severity over the years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been there since I was little. Like I said, my mom still has those notes from elementary school where teachers wrote that I was always out of my seat, talking before they called on me, and rushing through group stuff even when I understood the work. It’s changed some over time, but it wasn’t something that just started recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks not just in school, but also when working on your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. If a teacher or someone in a vendor meeting gives me a few steps, I’ll usually do the first part, but then later I find out I skipped something important, like attaching a form or finishing the last part of an order note.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your accessories shop, similar to what you experience in school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I know I miss steps sometimes, but with tiny details or careless mistakes specifically, I’d have to think about it more. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with organization, restlessness, and impulsivity are causing problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. Like, I missed some required steps for my program, and now my financial aid is kind of at risk because of it. And with my shop projects, people have started depending on me less because I don’t always follow through the way I’m supposed to. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in both your schoolwork and your accessories shop?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure if I’d say that happens often in both. I know I miss steps or forget parts of instructions sometimes, but with details or careless mistakes specifically, I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I think people are kind of losing trust in me. With my shop projects, they don’t depend on me as much now because I haven’t followed through on things. And outside of that, I missed required steps for my program, so now my financial aid is at risk. It’s been causing real problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as significant as your problems with organization and paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restlessness is pretty significant too. Even if I’m not actually going anywhere, I feel like my body is kind of buzzing, like I need to move. It’s especially bad when I’m supposed to just sit still and listen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’d have to think about it more.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your difficulties with organization, restlessness, and impulsivity caused significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"Because of all this, I missed required steps for my program and my financial aid is now at risk, and people I work with on shop projects have started depending on me less.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
